## 32.4.3. Access Control to seed

The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from U, S, or HS modes (see Privileged ISA specification).

Systems  should  implement  carefully  considered  access  control  policies  from  lower  privilege  modes  to physical entropy sources. The system can trap attempted access to seed and feed a less privileged client virtual entropy source data (Section 32.4.2.3) instead of invoking an SP 800-90B (Section 32.4.2.1) or PTG.2 (Section  32.4.2.2) physical  entropy  source .  Emulated seed data  generation  is  made  with  an  appropriately seeded, secure software DRBG. See Section 32.7.3.5 for security considerations related to direct access to entropy sources.

Implementations may implement mseccfg such that [s,u]seed is  a  read-only constant value 0 .  Software may discover if access to the seed CSR can be enabled in U and S mode by writing a 1 to [s,u]seed and reading back the result.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR.

RULE 2: The SSEED and USEED fields of mseccfg control access to the seed CSR from U, S, and HS modes.

RULE 3: Systems using the mseccfg access-control fields should implement carefully considered policies governing lower-privilege access to physical entropy sources.

RULE 4: Instead of permitting direct access through mseccfg to an SP 800-90B or PTG.2 physical entropy source, a system may trap an attempted seed CSR access and provide virtual entropy-source data to the less-privileged client.

RULE 5: When access controlled by mseccfg is trapped and seed data is emulated, the emulated data must be generated using an appropriately seeded, secure software DRBG.

RULE 6: An implementation may implement the SSEED and USEED fields of mseccfg as read-only fields whose constant value is 0.

RULE 7: If the SSEED or USEED field of mseccfg is a read-only constant 0, access to the seed CSR cannot be enabled for the corresponding lower-privilege mode.

RULE 8: Software can determine whether mseccfg permits seed CSR access to be enabled in U mode by writing 1 to USEED and reading back the value.

RULE 9: Software can determine whether mseccfg permits seed CSR access to be enabled in S mode by writing 1 to SSEED and reading back the value.

RULE 10: If writing 1 to a relevant SSEED or USEED field of mseccfg reads back as 1, software can infer that seed CSR access can be enabled for the corresponding mode; if it reads back as 0, it cannot be enabled.

## 32.7.3.5. Security Considerations for Direct Hardware Access

## Section 32.4.3.

The  ISA  implementation  and  system  design  must  try  to  ensure  that  the  hardware-software  interface minimizes avenues for adversarial information flow even if not explicitly forbidden in the specification.

For security, virtualization requires both conditioning and DRBG processing of physical entropy output. It is recommended if a single physical entropy source is shared between multiple different virtual machines or if the guest OS is untrusted. A virtual entropy source is significantly more resistant to depletion attacks and also lessens the risk from covert channels.

The  direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option  allows  one  to  draw  a  security  boundary  around  a  component  in relation  to  Sensitive  Security  Parameter  (SSP)  flows,  even  if  that  component  is  not  in  M  mode.  This  is helpful when implementing trusted enclaves. Such modules can enforce the entire key lifecycle from birth (in the entropy source) to death (zeroization) to occur without the key being passed across the boundary to external code.

Depletion. Active polling may deny the entropy source to another simultaneously running consumer. This can (for example) delay the instantiation of that virtual machine if it requires entropy to initialize fully.

Covert  Channels. Direct  access  to  a  component  such  as  the  entropy  source  can  be  used  to  establish communication  channels  across  security  boundaries.  Active  polling  from  one  consumer  makes  the resource unavailable WAIT instead of ES16 to another (which is polling infrequently). Such interactions can be used to establish low-bandwidth channels.

Hardware  Fingerprinting. An  entropy  source  (and  its  noise  source  circuits)  may  have  a  uniquely identifiable  hardware  "signature."  This  can  be  harmless  or  even  useful  in  some  applications  (as  random sources may exhibit Physically Un-clonable Function (PUF) -like features) but highly undesirable in others

(anonymized virtualized environments and enclaves). A DRBG masks such statistical features.

Side Channels. Some of the most devastating practical attacks against real-life cryptosystems have used inconsequential-looking additional information, such as padding error messages (Bardou et al., 2012) or timing information (Moghimi et al., 2020).

We urge implementers against creating unnecessary information flows via status or custom bits or to allow any other mechanism to disable or affect the entropy source output. All information flows and interaction mechanisms must be considered from an adversarial viewpoint: the fewer the better.

As an example of side-channel analysis, we note that the entropy polling interface is typically not "constant time." One needs to analyze what kind of information is revealed via the timing oracle; one way of doing it is to model seed as a rejection sampler. Such a timing oracle can reveal information about the noise source type  and  entropy  source  usage,  but  not  about  the  random  output entropy bits  themselves.  If  it  does, additional countermeasures are necessary.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option allows a security boundary to be established around a component with respect to Sensitive Security Parameter (SSP) flows.

RULE 2: A component protected using the direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option does not need to operate in M mode.

RULE 3: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option is useful when implementing trusted enclaves.

RULE 4: The direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option can allow a module to enforce the entire lifecycle of a key within its security boundary.

RULE 5: With the direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option, the protected key lifecycle can begin at key birth in the entropy source and end at key death through zeroization.

RULE 6: With the direct mseccfg.[s,u]seed option, a module can avoid passing a key across its security boundary to external code.

RULE 7: Direct entropy-source access enabled through mseccfg.[s,u]seed must be evaluated for depletion attacks because active polling can deny entropy to another simultaneous consumer.

RULE 8: In a virtualized system using mseccfg.[s,u]seed for direct entropy access, depletion can delay the initialization of another virtual machine that requires entropy.

RULE 9: Direct entropy-source access through mseccfg.[s,u]seed must be evaluated for covert channels because one consumer can alter whether another consumer observes WAIT instead of ES16.

RULE 10: Interactions resulting from mseccfg.[s,u]seed direct access can potentially establish low-bandwidth communication channels across security boundaries.

RULE 11: Direct entropy-source access through mseccfg.[s,u]seed must account for hardware fingerprinting because an entropy source and its noise-source circuits may possess a uniquely identifiable hardware signature.

RULE 12: When mseccfg.[s,u]seed is used in anonymized virtualized environments or enclaves, a DRBG can mask statistical features that could otherwise reveal an entropy-source hardware signature.

RULE 13: When mseccfg.[s,u]seed permits direct access to a physical entropy source shared among multiple virtual machines, the output should undergo conditioning and DRBG processing.

RULE 14: Conditioning and DRBG processing are recommended with mseccfg.[s,u]seed when the guest operating system receiving entropy is untrusted.

RULE 15: A virtual entropy source used in conjunction with mseccfg access is more resistant to depletion attacks and reduces covert-channel risk compared with unmediated physical entropy access.

RULE 16: Implementations of mseccfg.[s,u]seed should avoid unnecessary status bits, custom bits, or other mechanisms that create information flows or disable or affect entropy-source output.

RULE 17: All information flows and interaction mechanisms associated with mseccfg.[s,u]seed must be evaluated from an adversarial perspective, and their number should be minimized.

RULE 18: The entropy-polling interface exposed through mseccfg.[s,u]seed should not be assumed to operate in constant time.

RULE 19: Timing behavior associated with mseccfg.[s,u]seed polling must be analyzed as a possible timing oracle, such as by modeling seed generation as a rejection sampler.

RULE 20: A timing oracle associated with mseccfg.[s,u]seed may reveal the noise-source type and entropy-source usage.

RULE 21: A timing oracle associated with mseccfg.[s,u]seed must not reveal the random output entropy bits themselves; if it does, additional countermeasures are required.

## 3.1.19. Machine Security Configuration ( mseccfg ) Register

mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 26, that controls security features. It exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented. Otherwise, it is reserved.

Figure 26. Machine security configuration ( mseccfg ) register.

<image>

The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to the mseccfg CSR to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.

When USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception. When USEED is 1,  read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegalinstruction exception. If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, USEED is read-only zero.

When SSEED is  0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception. When SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception. If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, SSEED is read-only zero.

When the H extension is also implemented, access to the seed CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to  a  virtual-instruction  exception  in  VS  and  VU  modes;  all  other  types  of  accesses  raise  an  illegalinstruction exception.

Table 20. Entropy Source Access Control.

| Mode   | SSEED   | USEED   | Description                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
|--------|---------|---------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| M      | -       | -       | The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal (with a CSR read- write instruction.) Attempted read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits. |
| U      | -       | 0       | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.                                                                                                                                                        |
| U      | -       | 1       | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write.                                                                                                                                        |
| S/HS   | 0       | -       | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.                                                                                                                                                        |
| S/HS   | 1       | -       | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write.                                                                                                                                        |
| VS/VU  | 0       | -       | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception.                                                                                                                                                        |
| VS/VU  | 1       | -       | A read-write seed access raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception.                                                                              |

The Smepmp extension adds the RLB , MMWP , and the MML fields in mseccfg .

When mseccfg.RLB (Rule Locking Bypass) a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules. When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules  may be edited. When mseccfg.RLB is  0  and pmpcfg.L is  1  in  any  rule  or  entry  (including  disabled entries),  then mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are  ignored  until  a PMP reset .

<image>

This  feature  is  intended  to  be  used  as  a  debug  mechanism,  or  as  a  temporary  workaround

during the boot process for simplifying software, and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP  rules.  Using  this  functionality  under  normal  operation,  after  the  boot  process  is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules. Vendors who don't need this functionality may hardwire this field to 0.

The  terminology  used  to  specify  the  fields  introduced  by  the  Smepmp  extension  is  listed  in Chapter 6.

The mseccfg.MMWP (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy) is a WARL field. This field changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule. This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset . When set it changes the default PMP policy  for  M-mode  when  accessing  memory  regions  that  don't  have  a  matching PMP  rule ,  to denied instead of ignored .

The mseccfg.MML (Machine Mode Lockdown) is a WARL field. The MML bit changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit defined in Section 3.7.1.2. This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset . When mseccfg.MML is set the system's behavior changes in the following way:

1. The meaning of pmpcfg.L changes: Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset. The formerly reserved encoding of pmpcfg.RW=01 , and the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 , now encode a Shared-Region .

An M-mode-only rule  is enforced on  Machine mode and denied in  Supervisor  or  User  mode.  It  also remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset , unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.

A Shared-Region rule  is enforced on  all  modes,  with  restrictions  depending  on  the pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X bits:

- ⚫ A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U-mode has read access if pmpcfg.X is not set, or read/write access if pmpcfg.X is set.
- ⚫ A Shared-Region rule where pmpcfg.L is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/Umode, so is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and Mmode  also  has  read  access  if pmpcfg.X is  set.  The  rule  remains locked so  that  any  further modifications  to  its  associated  configuration  or  address  registers  are  ignored  until  a PMP reset , unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
- ⚫ The encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can  be  used  for  sharing  data  between  M-mode  and  S/U  mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset , unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
2. Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible  and  such pmpcfg writes  are  ignored,  leaving pmpcfg unchanged.  This  restriction  can  be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB e.g. during the boot process.
3. Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied .
4. If mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination of pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.

If  the Smmpm extension is implemented, the PMM field enables or disables pointer masking (see Chapter 25) for M-mode according to the values in Table 21. If Smmpm is not implemented, PMM is read-only zero. The PMM field is read-only zero for RV32.

Table 21. Legal values of PMM WARL field

|   Value | Description                                                            |
|---------|------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|      00 | Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0)                                |
|      01 | Reserved                                                               |
|      10 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64)  |
|      11 | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64) |

<image>

Smmpm implementations  need  to  satisfy  max(largest  supported  virtual  address  size,  largest supported supervisor physical address size) ⇐ (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.

The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg .  When MLPE field is 1, Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode. When the MLPE field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode and the following rules apply to M-mode.

- ⚫ The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as NO\_LP\_EXPECTED .
- ⚫ The LPAD instruction operates as a no-op.

When XLEN=32 only, mseccfgh is  a  32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg .  Register mseccfgh exists when XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write CSR that controls security features.

RULE 2: mseccfg is implemented if any implemented extension adds a field to mseccfg; otherwise, mseccfg is reserved.

RULE 3: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to mseccfg to control access to the seed CSR from privilege modes less privileged than M-mode.

RULE 4: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, any U-mode access to the seed CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 5: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, a read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed without an exception, while every other type of access raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 6: If either Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read-only zero.

RULE 7: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any S-mode or HS-mode access to the seed CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 8: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write access to the seed CSR from S-mode or HS-mode is allowed without an exception, while every other type of access raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 9: If either Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read-only zero.

RULE 10: Regardless of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED, the seed CSR is always available in M-mode through a normal CSR read-write instruction.

RULE 11: Regardless of privilege mode and the values of mseccfg.SSEED and mseccfg.USEED, an attempted read of the seed CSR without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 12: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any VS-mode or VU-mode access to the seed CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 13: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write seed CSR access from an HS-qualified instruction in VS-mode or VU-mode raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other seed CSR access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 14: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields to mseccfg.

RULE 15: mseccfg.RLB, named Rule Locking Bypass, is a WARL field that provides a mechanism for temporarily modifying locked PMP rules.

RULE 16: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed, modified, or edited.

RULE 17: If mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry, including a disabled entry, mseccfg.RLB remains 0 and subsequent attempts to modify mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.

RULE 18: mseccfg.RLB is intended for debugging or as a temporary boot-time workaround that simplifies software and optimizes allocation of memory and PMP rules.

RULE 19: Use of mseccfg.RLB during normal operation after boot should be avoided because it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.

RULE 20: An implementation that does not need Rule Locking Bypass functionality may hardwire mseccfg.RLB to 0.

RULE 21: mseccfg.MMWP, named Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy, is a WARL field.

RULE 22: mseccfg.MMWP is sticky: after it is set, it cannot be cleared until a PMP reset.

RULE 23: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, an M-mode access to a memory region with no matching PMP rule is denied rather than ignored.

RULE 24: mseccfg.MML, named Machine Mode Lockdown, is a WARL field.

RULE 25: mseccfg.MML is sticky: after it is set, it cannot be cleared until a PMP reset.

RULE 26: When mseccfg.MML is set, pmpcfg.L no longer simply marks a rule as locked and enforced in all modes; subject to the Shared-Region encodings, a set pmpcfg.L denotes an M-mode-only rule and a clear pmpcfg.L denotes an S/U-mode-only rule.

RULE 27: When mseccfg.MML is set, the formerly reserved pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding and the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encoding represent Shared-Region rules.

RULE 28: Under mseccfg.MML, an M-mode-only PMP rule is enforced for M-mode and denies access from Supervisor and User modes.

RULE 29: Under mseccfg.MML, an M-mode-only PMP rule remains locked, so changes to its associated PMP configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

RULE 30: Under mseccfg.MML, an S/U-mode-only PMP rule is enforced for Supervisor and User modes and denies access from M-mode.

RULE 31: Under mseccfg.MML, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L clear is a non-executable data-sharing region between M-mode and S/U-mode.

RULE 32: Under mseccfg.MML, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L clear gives M-mode read/write access; it gives S/U-mode read-only access when pmpcfg.X is clear and read/write access when pmpcfg.X is set.

RULE 33: Under mseccfg.MML, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L set is a non-writable code-sharing region between M-mode and S/U-mode.

RULE 34: Under mseccfg.MML, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L set gives both M-mode and S/U-mode execute access, and additionally gives M-mode read access when pmpcfg.X is set.

RULE 35: Under mseccfg.MML, a Shared-Region rule with pmpcfg.L set remains locked, so changes to its associated PMP configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

RULE 36: Under mseccfg.MML, the pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 Shared-Region encoding gives both M-mode and S/U-mode read-only access to the region.

RULE 37: Under mseccfg.MML, a pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 Shared-Region rule remains locked, so changes to its associated PMP configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.

RULE 38: When mseccfg.MML is set and mseccfg.RLB is not providing a bypass, software cannot add executable privileges to an M-mode-only rule or to a locked Shared-Region rule; attempted pmpcfg writes that do so are ignored and leave pmpcfg unchanged.

RULE 39: Setting mseccfg.RLB temporarily lifts the mseccfg.MML restriction against adding executable M-mode-only rules or executable locked Shared-Region rules, for example during boot.

RULE 40: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode code may execute only from a region matching an executable M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.

RULE 41: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode execution from a region without a matching PMP rule is denied.

RULE 42: When mseccfg.MML is set, M-mode execution from a region matching an S/U-mode-only rule is denied.

RULE 43: When mseccfg.MML is clear, the pmpcfg.RW=01 encoding remains reserved for future standard use.

RULE 44: If the Smmpm extension is implemented, mseccfg.PMM is a WARL field that enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode.

RULE 45: If Smmpm is not implemented, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.

RULE 46: On RV32, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.

RULE 47: A value of 00 in mseccfg.PMM disables pointer masking and selects PMLEN=0.

RULE 48: A value of 01 in mseccfg.PMM is reserved.

RULE 49: A value of 10 in mseccfg.PMM enables pointer masking with PMLEN=XLEN−57, which gives PMLEN=7 on RV64.

RULE 50: A value of 11 in mseccfg.PMM enables pointer masking with PMLEN=XLEN−48, which gives PMLEN=16 on RV64.

RULE 51: An Smmpm implementation using mseccfg.PMM must satisfy max(largest supported virtual-address size, largest supported supervisor physical-address size) ≤ XLEN−PMLEN bits to avoid masking logic on the TLB access path.

RULE 52: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field to mseccfg.

RULE 53: When mseccfg.MLPE is 1, Zicfilp is enabled in M-mode.

RULE 54: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, Zicfilp is not enabled in M-mode.

RULE 55: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the hart does not update the ELP state in M-mode, and the ELP state remains NO_LP_EXPECTED.

RULE 56: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op in M-mode.

RULE 57: When XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.

RULE 58: When XLEN=64, mseccfgh does not exist, even when mseccfg is implemented.

## 3.4. Reset

Upon reset, a hart's privilege mode is set to M. The mstatus fields MIE and MPRV are reset to 0. If littleendian memory accesses are supported, the mstatus / mstatush field MBE is reset to 0. The misa register is reset to enable the maximal set of supported extensions, as described in Section 3.1.1. For implementations with  the  "A"  standard  extension,  there  is  no  valid  load  reservation.  The pc is  set  to  an  implementationdefined reset vector. The mcause register is set to a value indicating the cause of the reset. Writable PMP registers' A and L fields are set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value for some PMP registers' A and L fields. If the hypervisor extension is implemented, the hgatp .MODE and vsatp .MODE fields are reset to 0. If the Smrnmi extension is implemented, the mnstatus .NMIE field is reset to 0. No WARL field  contains an illegal value. If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg .MLPE field is reset to 0. All other hart state is UNSPECIFIED.

The MML , MMWP , and RLB fields of the mseccfg register are set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

The mcause values  after  reset  have  implementation-specific  interpretation,  but  the  value  0  should  be returned  on  implementations  that  do  not  distinguish  different  reset  conditions.  Implementations  that distinguish different reset conditions should only use 0 to indicate the most complete reset.

The USEED and SSEED fields of the mseccfg CSR must have defined reset values. The system must not allow them to be in an undefined state after reset.

<image>

Some  designs  may  have  multiple  causes  of  reset  (e.g.,  power-on  reset,  external  hard  reset, brownout  detected,  watchdog  timer  elapse,  sleep-mode  wake-up),  which  machine-mode software and debuggers may wish to distinguish.

To  avoid  ambiguity, mcause reset  values  may  alias mcause values  following  synchronous transition to a lower power state, for example.

The same "wait-for-event" template might be used for possible future extensions that wait on memory locations changing, or message arrival.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg.MLPE field must be reset to 0.

RULE 2: The mseccfg.MML field must be reset to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 3: The mseccfg.MMWP field must be reset to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 4: The mseccfg.RLB field must be reset to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

RULE 5: The mseccfg.USEED field must have a defined reset value and must not be left in an undefined state after reset.

RULE 6: The mseccfg.SSEED field must have a defined reset value and must not be left in an undefined state after reset.

## 23.1.1. Landing-Pad-Enabled (LPE) State

The  term xLPE is  used  to  determine  if  forward-edge  CFI  using  landing  pads  provided  by  the  Zicfilp extension is enabled at a privilege mode.

When S-mode is implemented, it is determined as follows:

Table 58. xLPE determination when S-mode is implemented

| Privilege Mode   | xLPE         |
|------------------|--------------|
| M                | mseccfg.MLPE |
| S or HS          | menvcfg.LPE  |
| VS               | henvcfg.LPE  |
| Uor VU           | senvcfg.LPE  |

When S-mode is not implemented, it is determined as follows:

Table 59. xLPE determination when S-mode is not implemented

| Privilege Mode   | xLPE         |
|------------------|--------------|
| M                | mseccfg.MLPE |
| U                | menvcfg.LPE  |

The Zicfilp must be explicitly enabled for use at each privilege mode.

Programs  compiled  with  the LPAD instruction  continue  to  function  correctly,  but  without forward-edge CFI protection, when the Zicfilp extension is not implemented or is not enabled.

<image>
============================================================

RESPONSE:

RULE 2: When S-mode is implemented, M-mode xLPE equals mseccfg.MLPE.

RULE 3: When S-mode is not implemented, M-mode xLPE still equals mseccfg.MLPE.

RULE 4: mseccfg.MLPE controls whether forward-edge control-flow integrity using Zicfilp landing pads is enabled in M-mode.

RULE 5: The Zicfilp extension must be explicitly enabled for M-mode through mseccfg.MLPE.

RULE 6: If Zicfilp is not implemented or is not enabled in M-mode by mseccfg.MLPE, programs containing LPAD instructions continue to function correctly but do not receive forward-edge CFI protection in M-mode.

The text below:

## 14.1. Wait-on-Reservation-Set Instructions

The WRS.NTO and WRS.STO instructions cause the hart to temporarily stall execution in a low-power state as long as the reservation set is valid and no pending interrupts, even if disabled, are observed. For WRS.STO the stall duration is bounded by an implementation defined short timeout. These instructions are available in all privilege modes.

<image>

Hart execution may be stalled while the following conditions are all satisfied:

- a. The reservation set is valid
- b. If WRS.STO , a "short" duration since start of stall has not elapsed
- c. No pending interrupt is observed (see the rules below)

While stalled, an implementation is permitted to occasionally terminate the stall and complete execution for any reason.

WRS.NTO and WRS.STO instructions  follow  the  rules  of  the WFI instruction  for  resuming  execution  on  a pending interrupt.

When the TW (Timeout Wait) bit in mstatus is set and WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific  bounded  time  limit,  the WRS.NTO instruction will cause an illegal-instruction exception.

When executing in VS or VU mode, if the VTW bit is set in hstatus ,  the TW bit in mstatus is clear, and the WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, the WRS.NTO instruction will cause a virtual-instruction exception.

Since the WRS.STO and WRS.NTO instructions can complete execution for reasons other than stores  to  the  reservation  set,  software  will  likely  need  a  means  of  looping  until  the  required stores have occurred.

<image>

The  duration  of  a WRS.STO instruction's  timeout  may  vary  significantly  within  and  among implementations. In typical implementations this duration should be roughly in the range of 10 to 100 times an on-chip cache miss latency or a cacheless access to main memory.

WRS.NTO , unlike WFI , is not specified to cause an illegal-instruction exception if executed in Umode when the governing TW bit is 0. WFI is typically not expected to be used in U-mode and on many systems may promptly cause an illegal-instruction exception if used at U-mode. Unlike WFI , WRS.NTO is  expected  to  be  used  by  software  in  U-mode  when  waiting  on  memory  but without a deadline for that wait.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register contains the TW (Timeout Wait) bit, which governs timeout behavior for WRS.NTO outside M-mode.

RULE 2: If mstatus.TW is set, WRS.NTO is executed in any privilege mode other than M-mode, and the instruction does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, WRS.NTO must raise an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 3: The mstatus.TW rule for an illegal-instruction exception does not apply when WRS.NTO is executed in M-mode.

RULE 4: When WRS.NTO is executed in VS-mode or VU-mode, if mstatus.TW is clear, hstatus.VTW is set, and WRS.NTO does not complete within an implementation-specific bounded time limit, WRS.NTO must raise a virtual-instruction exception.

RULE 5: When mstatus.TW is clear, WRS.NTO is not specified to raise an illegal-instruction exception merely because it is executed in U-mode.

RULE 6: The described mstatus.TW timeout and exception rules apply to WRS.NTO; the text does not specify corresponding mstatus.TW behavior for WRS.STO.

The text below:

## 20.4.2.2. Page-Fault, Guest-Page-Fault, and Access-Fault Exceptions

Similar  to  load  and  store  instructions,  CMO  instructions  are  explicit  memory  access  instructions  that compute an effective address. The effective address is ultimately translated into a physical address based on  the  privilege  mode  and  the  enabled  translation  mechanisms,  and  the  CMO  extensions  impose  the following constraints on the physical addresses in a given cache block:

- ⚫ The PMP access control bits shall be the same for all physical addresses in the cache block, and if write permission is granted by the PMP access control bits, read permission shall also be granted
- ⚫ The PMAs shall be the same for all physical addresses in the cache block, and if write permission is granted by the supported access type PMAs, read permission shall also be granted

If the above constraints are not met, the behavior of a CBO instruction is UNSPECIFIED.

<image>

This specification assumes that the above constraints will typically be met for main memory regions and may be met for certain I/O regions.

<image>

The access size for CMO instructions is equal to the size of the cache block, however in some cases  that  access  can  be  decomposed  into  multiple  memory  operations.  PMP  checks  are applied to each memory operation independently. For example a 64-byte cbo.zero that spans two  32-byte  PMP  regions  would  succeed  if  it  was  decomposed  into  two  32-byte  memory operations (and the PMP access control bits are the same in both regions), but if performed as a single 64-byte memory operation it would cause an access fault.

The  Zicboz  extension  introduces  an  additional  supported  access  type  PMA  for  cache-block  zero instructions.  Main  memory  regions  are  required  to  support  accesses  by  cache-block  zero  instructions; however, I/O regions may specify whether accesses by cache-block zero instructions are supported.

A cache-block management instruction is permitted to access the specified cache block whenever a load instruction or store instruction is permitted to access the corresponding physical addresses. If neither a load instruction nor store instruction is permitted to access the physical addresses, but an instruction fetch is permitted to access the physical addresses, whether a cache-block management instruction is permitted to  access  the  cache  block  is  UNSPECIFIED.  If  access  to  the  cache  block  is  not  permitted,  a  cache-block management instruction raises a store page-fault or store guest-page-fault exception if address translation does not permit any access or raises a store access-fault exception otherwise. During address translation, the instruction also checks the accessed bit and may either raise an exception or set the bit as required.

<image>

The interaction between cache-block management instructions and instruction fetches will be specified in a future extension.

As implied by omission, a cache-block management instruction does not check the dirty bit and neither raises an exception nor sets the bit.

A cache-block zero instruction is permitted to access the specified cache block whenever a store instruction is permitted to access the corresponding physical addresses and when the PMAs indicate that cache-block zero instructions are a supported access type. If access to the cache block is not permitted, a cache-block zero instruction raises a store page-fault or store guest-page-fault exception if address translation does not permit  write  access  or  raises  a  store  access-fault  exception  otherwise.  During  address  translation,  the instruction  also  checks  the  accessed  and  dirty  bits  and  may  either  raise  an  exception  or  set  the  bits  as required.

A  cache-block  prefetch  instruction  is  permitted  to  access  the  specified  cache  block  whenever  a  load instruction,  store  instruction,  or  instruction  fetch  is  permitted  to  access  the  corresponding  physical addresses. If access to the cache block is not permitted, a cache-block prefetch instruction does not raise any  exceptions  and  shall  not  access  any  caches  or  memory.  During  address  translation,  the  instruction does not check the accessed and dirty bits and neither raises an exception nor sets the bits.

When a page-fault, guest-page-fault, or access-fault exception is taken, the relevant *tval CSR is written with the faulting effective address (i.e. the value of rs1 ).

<image>

Like a load or store instruction, a CMO instruction may or may not be permitted to access a cache block based on the states of the MPRV , MPV , and MPP bits in mstatus and the SUM and MXR bits in mstatus , sstatus , and vsstatus .

This  specification  expects  that  implementations  will  process  cache-block  management instructions like store/AMO instructions, so store/AMO exceptions are appropriate for these instructions, regardless of the permissions required.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: A CMO instruction's permission to access a cache block may depend on the states of the MPRV, MPV, MPP, SUM, and MXR bits associated with mstatus.

RULE 2: The MPRV bit in mstatus can affect whether a CMO instruction is permitted to access a cache block.

RULE 3: The MPV bit in mstatus can affect whether a CMO instruction is permitted to access a cache block.

RULE 4: The MPP bits in mstatus can affect whether a CMO instruction is permitted to access a cache block.

RULE 5: The SUM bit in mstatus, sstatus, or vsstatus can affect whether a CMO instruction is permitted to access a cache block.

RULE 6: The MXR bit in mstatus, sstatus, or vsstatus can affect whether a CMO instruction is permitted to access a cache block.

RULE 7: The effects of the relevant mstatus bits on CMO cache-block access permissions are analogous to their effects on load and store instruction access permissions.

## 27.6. Privileged Architecture Implications

In the standard privileged architecture defined in Volume II, the mstatus field FS is hardwired to 0 if the

Zfinx  extension  is  implemented,  and  FS  no  longer  affects  the  trapping  behavior  of  floating-point instructions or fcsr accesses.

The misa bits F, D, and Q are hardwired to 0 when the Zfinx extension is implemented.

<image>

A future discoverability mechanism might be used to probe the existence of the Zfinx, Zhinx, and Zdinx extensions.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus FS field is hardwired to 0 when the Zfinx extension is implemented.

RULE 2: When the Zfinx extension is implemented, the mstatus FS field no longer affects the trapping behavior of floating-point instructions.

RULE 3: When the Zfinx extension is implemented, the mstatus FS field no longer affects the trapping behavior of fcsr accesses.

## 31.3.2. Vector Context Status in mstatus

A vector context status field, VS , is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9] . It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS .

Attempts  to  execute  any  vector  instruction,  or  to  access  the  vector  CSRs,  raise  an  illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.

When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the  vector  CSRs,  will  change mstatus.VS to  Dirty.  Implementations  may  also  change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

<image>

Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is  an  optimization.  Software  will  typically  use  VS  to  reduce context-swap overhead.

If mstatus.VS is  Dirty, mstatus.SD is  1;  otherwise, mstatus.SD is  set  in  accordance  with  existing specifications.

Implementations may have a writable misa.V field. Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.

<image>

Allowing mstatus.VS to  exist  when misa.V is  clear,  enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V .
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The vector context status field mstatus.VS occupies bits mstatus[10:9].

RULE 2: The mstatus.VS field is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].

RULE 3: The mstatus.VS field is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field mstatus.FS.

RULE 4: When mstatus.VS is Off, attempting to execute any vector instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 5: When mstatus.VS is Off, attempting to access any vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 6: When mstatus.VS is Initial or Clean, executing an instruction that changes vector state changes mstatus.VS to Dirty.

RULE 7: Changes to vector CSRs count as vector-state changes that cause mstatus.VS to become Dirty when it was Initial or Clean.

RULE 8: An implementation may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even if no vector state has changed.

RULE 9: Accurate tracking of mstatus.VS is an optimization rather than a strict implementation requirement.

RULE 10: Software typically uses mstatus.VS to reduce vector context-switch overhead.

RULE 11: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is set to 1.

RULE 12: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set according to the existing specifications.

RULE 13: The mstatus.VS field may exist even when the misa.V extension bit is clear.

RULE 14: The ability for mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear is analogous to the handling of the floating-point unit and its context-status field.

RULE 15: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist while misa.V is clear enables vector emulation.

RULE 16: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist while misa.V is clear simplifies mstatus.VS handling in systems that implement misa.V as a writable field.

## 31.3.3. Vector Context Status in vsstatus

When the hypervisor extension is present, a vector context status field, VS , is added to vsstatus[10:9] . It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS .

When V=1, both vsstatus.VS and mstatus.VS are in effect: attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when either field is set to Off.

When V=1 and neither vsstatus.VS nor mstatus.VS is  set  to  Off,  executing any instruction that changes vector  state,  including  the  vector  CSRs,  will  change  both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty. Implementations may also change mstatus.VS or vsstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.

If vsstatus.VS is  Dirty, vsstatus.SD is  1;  otherwise, vsstatus.SD is  set  in  accordance  with  existing specifications.

If mstatus.VS is  Dirty, mstatus.SD is  1;  otherwise, mstatus.SD is  set  in  accordance  with  existing specifications.

For implementations with a writable misa.V field, the vsstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: When V=1, both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS are in effect.

RULE 2: When V=1, executing any vector instruction or accessing any vector CSR raises an illegal-instruction exception if mstatus.VS is Off, regardless of the value of vsstatus.VS.

RULE 3: When V=1, executing any vector instruction or accessing any vector CSR also raises an illegal-instruction exception if vsstatus.VS is Off, even if mstatus.VS is not Off.

RULE 4: When V=1 and neither mstatus.VS nor vsstatus.VS is Off, any instruction that changes vector state, including an instruction that changes a vector CSR, sets both mstatus.VS and vsstatus.VS to Dirty.

RULE 5: An implementation may change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when no vector state has changed.

RULE 6: An implementation may change vsstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty independently of an actual vector-state change, while mstatus.VS is governed by the same implementation latitude.

RULE 7: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.

RULE 8: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set according to the existing specifications.

## 31.13. Vector Floating-Point Instructions

The standard vector floating-point instructions treat elements as IEEE-754/2008-compatible values. If the EEW of a vector floating-point operand does not correspond to a supported IEEE floating-point type, the instruction encoding is reserved.

<image>

Whether  floating-point  is  supported,  and  for  which  element  widths,  is  determined  by  the specific vector extension. The current set of extensions include support for 32-bit and 64-bit floating-point values. When 16-bit and 128-bit element widths are added, they will be also be treated as IEEE-754/2008-compatible values. Other floating-point formats may be supported in future extensions.

Vector floating-point instructions require the presence of base scalar floating-point extensions corresponding to the supported vector floating-point element widths.

<image>

In  particular,  future  vector  extensions  supporting  16-bit  half-precision  floating-point  values will also require some scalar half-precision floating-point support.

If the floating-point unit status field mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception. Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies any floating-point extension state (i.e., floating-point CSRs or f registers) must set mstatus.FS to Dirty .

If  the  hypervisor  extension  is  implemented  and  V=1,  the vsstatus.FS field  is  additionally  in  effect  for vector floating-point instructions. If vsstatus.FS or mstatus.FS is Off then any attempt to execute a vector floating-point instruction will raise an illegal-instruction exception. Any vector floating-point instruction that  modifies  any  floating-point  extension  state  (i.e.,  floating-point  CSRs  or f registers)  must  set  both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty .

The vector floating-point instructions have the same behavior as the scalar floating-point instructions with regard to NaNs.

Scalar values for floating-point vector-scalar operations are sourced as described in Section 31.10.1.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: If mstatus.FS is Off, attempting to execute any vector floating-point instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception.

RULE 2: Any vector floating-point instruction that modifies floating-point extension state must set mstatus.FS to Dirty.

RULE 3: Floating-point extension state relevant to setting mstatus.FS to Dirty includes floating-point CSRs and floating-point f registers.

RULE 4: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS apply to vector floating-point instructions.

RULE 5: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, a vector floating-point instruction raises an illegal-instruction exception when either mstatus.FS or vsstatus.FS is Off.

RULE 6: If the hypervisor extension is implemented and V=1, any vector floating-point instruction that modifies floating-point CSRs or f registers must set both mstatus.FS and vsstatus.FS to Dirty.
======================================================= END - 13 =======================================================

## 3.1.6.1. Privilege and Global Interrupt-Enable Stack in mstatus register

Global interrupt-enable bits, MIE and SIE, are provided for M-mode and S-mode respectively. These bits are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

<image>

The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus , allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

When a hart  is  executing  in  privilege  mode x ,  interrupts  are  globally  enabled  when x IE=1  and  globally disabled when x IE=0. Interrupts for lower-privilege modes, w < x , are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global w IE bit for the lower-privilege mode. Interrupts for higher-privilege modes, y > x ,

0

are  always  globally  enabled  regardless  of  the  setting  of  the  global y IE  bit  for  the  higher-privilege  mode. Higher-privilege-level code can use separate per-interrupt enable bits to disable selected higher-privilegemode interrupts before ceding control to a lower-privilege mode. If supervisor mode is not implemented, then SIE and SPIE are read-only 0.

<image>

A higher-privilege mode y could disable all of its interrupts before ceding control to a lowerprivilege  mode  but  this  would  be  unusual  as  it  would  leave  only  a  synchronous  trap,  nonmaskable interrupt, or reset as means to regain control of the hart.

To  support  nested  traps,  each  privilege  mode x that  can  respond  to  interrupts  has  a  two-level  stack  of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes. x PIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap, and x PP holds the previous privilege mode. The x PP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x , so MPP is two bits wide and SPP is one bit wide. When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x , x PIE is set to the value of x IE; x IE is set to 0; and x PP is set to y .

<image>

For lower privilege modes, any trap (synchronous or asynchronous) is usually taken at a higher privilege mode with interrupts disabled upon entry. The higher-level trap handler will either service the trap and return using the stacked information, or, if not returning immediately to the interrupted context, will save the privilege stack before re-enabling interrupts, so only one entry per stack is required.

An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode respectively. When executing an x RET instruction, supposing x PP holds the value y , x IE is set to x PIE; the privilege mode is changed  to y ; x PIE  is  set  to  1;  and x PP  is  set  to  the  least-privileged  supported  mode  (U  if  U-mode  is implemented, else M). If y ≠M, x RET also sets MPRV=0.

<image>

Setting xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

<image>

Trap handlers must be designed to neither enable interrupts nor cause exceptions during the phase of handling where the trap handler preserves the critical state information required to handle  and  resume  from  the  trap.  An  exception  or  interrupt  in  this  critical  phase  of  trap handling may lead to a trap that can overwrite such critical state. This could result in the loss of data needed to recover from the initial trap. Further, if an exception occurs in the code path needed to handle traps, then such a situation may lead to an infinite loop of traps. To prevent this,  trap  handlers  must  be  meticulously  designed  to  identify  and  safely  manage  exceptions within their operational flow.

x PP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x . If privilege mode x is not implemented, then x PP must be read-only 0.

<image>

M-mode  software  can  determine  whether  a  privilege  mode  is  implemented  by  writing  that mode to MPP then reading it back.

If  the  machine  provides  only  U  and  M  modes,  then  only  a  single  hardware  storage  bit  is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus register provides the global interrupt-enable bits MIE for M-mode and SIE for S-mode.

RULE 2: The MIE and SIE bits in mstatus are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers executing in the current privilege mode.

RULE 3: The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing software to atomically set or clear them with a single CSR instruction.

RULE 4: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for that mode are globally enabled if the corresponding xIE bit in mstatus is 1.

RULE 5: When a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts for that mode are globally disabled if the corresponding xIE bit in mstatus is 0.

RULE 6: While a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts targeting any lower privilege mode w, where w < x, are globally disabled regardless of the lower mode's wIE setting in mstatus.

RULE 7: While a hart executes in privilege mode x, interrupts targeting any higher privilege mode y, where y > x, are globally enabled regardless of the higher mode's yIE setting in mstatus.

RULE 8: Higher-privilege software can use separate per-interrupt enable bits, rather than the global interrupt-enable fields in mstatus, to disable selected higher-privilege interrupts before transferring control to a lower privilege mode.

RULE 9: If S-mode is not implemented, the SIE and SPIE fields in mstatus are read-only zero.

RULE 10: A higher privilege mode can disable all of its interrupts through its mstatus interrupt-enable state before transferring control to a lower privilege mode, but doing so is unusual because only a synchronous trap, nonmaskable interrupt, or reset could then regain control of the hart.

RULE 11: For each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, mstatus provides a two-level stack of interrupt-enable state and privilege-mode state through xIE, xPIE, and xPP.

RULE 12: The xPIE field in mstatus stores the value of the xIE interrupt-enable bit that was active before a trap was taken.

RULE 13: The xPP field in mstatus stores the privilege mode that was active before a trap was taken.

RULE 14: An xPP field in mstatus can represent only privilege mode x and implemented privilege modes lower than x.

RULE 15: The MPP field in mstatus is two bits wide because it may need to represent privilege modes up to M-mode.

RULE 16: The SPP field in mstatus is one bit wide because it only needs to represent the previous privilege mode supported below or at S-mode.

RULE 17: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to the previous value of xIE.

RULE 18: When a trap is taken into privilege mode x, the xIE field in mstatus is cleared to zero, disabling interrupts for mode x upon trap entry.

RULE 19: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, the xPP field in mstatus is set to y.

RULE 20: Traps from lower privilege modes, whether synchronous or asynchronous, are usually taken in a higher privilege mode with interrupts disabled through the mstatus xIE state on entry.

RULE 21: A higher-level trap handler can service a trap and return using the state stacked in the mstatus xPIE and xPP fields.

RULE 22: If a higher-level trap handler does not immediately return to the interrupted context, it must save the privilege-stack information from mstatus before re-enabling interrupts.

RULE 23: Because software saves the mstatus privilege-stack information before re-enabling interrupts when necessary, only one hardware stack entry per privilege mode is required.

RULE 24: MRET returns from an M-mode trap using the M-mode trap-return state held in mstatus, while SRET returns from an S-mode trap using the S-mode trap-return state held in mstatus.

RULE 25: When an xRET instruction executes and xPP in mstatus contains privilege mode y, xIE is set to the value stored in xPIE.

RULE 26: When an xRET instruction executes and xPP in mstatus contains privilege mode y, the hart's current privilege mode is changed to y.

RULE 27: When an xRET instruction executes, the xPIE field in mstatus is set to 1.

RULE 28: When an xRET instruction executes, the xPP field in mstatus is set to the least-privileged supported mode, specified as U-mode if U-mode is implemented and otherwise M-mode.

RULE 29: If the privilege mode y restored by xRET is not M-mode, xRET also clears the MPRV field in mstatus to zero.

RULE 30: Resetting xPP in mstatus to the least-privileged supported mode on xRET helps identify software bugs involving incorrect management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

RULE 31: Trap handlers must not enable interrupts through mstatus while preserving critical state needed to handle and resume from the trap.

RULE 32: Trap handlers must avoid exceptions while preserving critical state associated with the trap and the mstatus privilege stack, because a nested trap could overwrite critical state.

RULE 33: If an interrupt or exception occurs during the critical trap-handling phase, state associated with the initial trap, including software-preserved mstatus stack information, can be lost.

RULE 34: Exceptions in a trap-handling path can cause an infinite loop of traps, so code managing mstatus trap state must safely account for exceptions in its operational flow.

RULE 35: The xPP fields in mstatus are WARL fields, meaning written values are constrained to privilege mode x and implemented privilege modes lower than x.

RULE 36: If privilege mode x is not implemented, its corresponding xPP field in mstatus must be read-only zero.

RULE 37: M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode's encoding to the MPP field in mstatus and then reading MPP back.

RULE 38: If a machine implements only U-mode and M-mode, the MPP field in mstatus needs only one hardware storage bit to distinguish the encodings 00 and 11.

The text below:

## 3.1.6.2. Double Trap Control in mstatus Register

A double trap typically arises during a sensitive phase in trap handling operations -when an exception or interrupt occurs while the trap handler (the component responsible for managing these events) is in a nonreentrant state. This non-reentrancy usually occurs in the early phase of trap handling, wherein the trap handler has not yet preserved the necessary state to handle and resume from the trap. The occurrence of a trap during this phase can lead to an overwrite of critical state information, resulting in the loss of data needed  to  recover  from  the  initial  trap.  The  trap  that  caused  this  critical  error  condition  is  henceforth

called the unexpected trap .  Trap  handlers are designed to neither enable interrupts nor cause exceptions during  this  phase  of  handling.  However,  managing  Hardware-Error  exceptions,  which  may  occur unpredictably, presents significant challenges in trap handler implementation due to the potential risk of a double trap.

The M-mode-disable-trap ( MDT ) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension. Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1. When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0. For RV64, this clearing occurs regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write. The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write (For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit in mstatus register).

When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit  is  currently  0,  it  is  then  set  to  1,  and  the  trap  is delivered as expected. However, if MDT is already set to 1, then this is an unexpected trap . When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit. A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit. However, a trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap .

In the event of a unexpected trap , the handling is as follows:

- ⚫ When  the  Smrnmi  extension  is  implemented  and mnstatus.NMIE is  1,  the  hart  traps  to  the  RNMI handler.  To  deliver  this  trap,  the mnepc and mncause registers  are  written  with  the  values  that  the unexpected trap would have written to the mepc and mcause registers respectively. The privilege mode information fields in the mnstatus register are written to indicate M-mode and its NMIE field is set to 0.

<image>

The consequence of this specification is that on occurrence of double trap the RNMI handler is not provided with information that a trap reports in the mtval and the mtval2 registers. This information, if needed, can be obtained by the RNMI handler by decoding the instruction at the address in mnepc and examining its source register contents.

- ⚫ When the  Smrnmi  extension  is  not  implemented,  or  if  the  Smrnmi  extension  is  implemented  and mnstatus.NMIE is  0,  the  hart  enters  a  critical-error  state  without  updating  any  architectural  state, including the pc .  This state involves ceasing execution, disabling all interrupts (including NMIs), and asserting  a critical-error signal  to  the  platform.  Whether  performance  counters  and  timers  are updated in the critical-error state is UNSPECIFIED.

<image>

The  actions  performed  by  the  platform  when  a  hart  asserts  a critical-error signal  are platform-specific.  The  range  of  possible  actions  include  restarting  the  affected  hart  or restarting the entire platform, among others.

The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M-mode, set the MDT bit to 0. If the new privilege mode is U, VS, or VU, then sstatus.SDT is also set to 0. Additionally, if it is VU, then vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M. If it is U, VS, or VU, then sstatus.SDT is also set to 0. Additionally, if it is VU, then vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The mstatus double-trap mechanism addresses traps that occur while a trap handler is in a nonreentrant phase and has not yet preserved the state required to recover from the original trap.

RULE 2: A trap occurring during the sensitive handling phase protected by mstatus may overwrite critical state and cause loss of the information required to recover from the initial trap.

RULE 3: In the mstatus double-trap mechanism, the trap that produces this critical error condition is called an unexpected trap.

RULE 4: Trap handlers using the mstatus double-trap mechanism should neither enable interrupts nor cause exceptions during their nonreentrant handling phase.

RULE 5: The mstatus double-trap mechanism is especially relevant to Hardware-Error exceptions because such exceptions can occur unpredictably during trap handling.

RULE 6: The MDT field associated with mstatus is the M-mode-disable-trap bit and is introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.

RULE 7: The MDT field associated with mstatus is a WARL field.

RULE 8: Upon reset, the MDT field associated with mstatus is set to 1.

RULE 9: When an explicit CSR write sets the mstatus MDT bit to 1, the mstatus MIE bit is cleared to 0.

RULE 10: On RV64, setting mstatus.MDT to 1 clears mstatus.MIE to 0 regardless of the value written to mstatus.MIE by the same CSR write.

RULE 11: The mstatus MIE bit can be explicitly set to 1 only when MDT is already 0 or, on RV64, when the same CSR write sets MDT to 0.

RULE 12: On RV32, MDT is located in mstatush while MIE is located in mstatus.

RULE 13: When a trap is taken into M-mode while the MDT field associated with mstatus is 0, MDT is set to 1 and the trap is delivered normally.

RULE 14: When a trap is to be taken into M-mode while the MDT field associated with mstatus is already 1, the trap is classified as an unexpected trap.

RULE 15: If the Smrnmi extension is implemented, an RNMI-caused trap is not an unexpected trap under the mstatus double-trap mechanism, regardless of the MDT value.

RULE 16: A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT field associated with mstatus.

RULE 17: A trap occurring during execution in M-mode while mnstatus.NMIE is 0 is an unexpected trap under the mstatus double-trap mechanism.

RULE 18: If an unexpected trap detected by the mstatus double-trap mechanism occurs while Smrnmi is implemented and mnstatus.NMIE is 1, the hart traps to the RNMI handler.

RULE 19: When the mstatus double-trap mechanism redirects an unexpected trap to the RNMI handler, mnepc receives the value that the trap would have written to mepc.

RULE 20: When the mstatus double-trap mechanism redirects an unexpected trap to the RNMI handler, mncause receives the value that the trap would have written to mcause.

RULE 21: When an unexpected trap detected by the mstatus double-trap mechanism is delivered to the RNMI handler, the privilege-mode fields in mnstatus are written to indicate M-mode.

RULE 22: When an unexpected trap detected by the mstatus double-trap mechanism is delivered to the RNMI handler, mnstatus.NMIE is set to 0.

RULE 23: For a double trap detected through the mstatus mechanism, the RNMI handler is not provided with the information that an ordinary trap would report in mtval and mtval2.

RULE 24: If the information omitted from mtval and mtval2 is required after an mstatus-detected double trap, the RNMI handler can obtain it by decoding the instruction at mnepc and examining the instruction's source-register contents.

RULE 25: If Smrnmi is not implemented, an unexpected trap detected by the mstatus double-trap mechanism causes the hart to enter a critical-error state.

RULE 26: If Smrnmi is implemented but mnstatus.NMIE is 0, an unexpected trap detected by the mstatus double-trap mechanism causes the hart to enter a critical-error state.

RULE 27: When an mstatus-detected unexpected trap causes a critical-error state, the hart does not update any architectural state, including the pc.

RULE 28: In the critical-error state caused by the mstatus double-trap mechanism, the hart ceases execution, disables all interrupts including NMIs, and asserts a critical-error signal to the platform.

RULE 29: Whether performance counters and timers continue to update in a critical-error state caused by the mstatus double-trap mechanism is UNSPECIFIED.

RULE 30: Platform handling of the critical-error signal produced by the mstatus double-trap mechanism is platform-specific and may include restarting the affected hart or restarting the entire platform.

RULE 31: An MRET instruction executed in M-mode sets the MDT field associated with mstatus to 0.

RULE 32: An SRET instruction executed in M-mode sets the MDT field associated with mstatus to 0.

RULE 33: When MRET or SRET clears the MDT field associated with mstatus and the new privilege mode is U, VS, or VU, sstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

RULE 34: When MRET or SRET clears the MDT field associated with mstatus and the new privilege mode is VU, vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

RULE 35: The MNRET instruction provided by Smrnmi sets the MDT field associated with mstatus to 0 when the new privilege mode is not M.

RULE 36: When MNRET clears the MDT field associated with mstatus and the new privilege mode is U, VS, or VU, sstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

RULE 37: When MNRET clears the MDT field associated with mstatus and the new privilege mode is VU, vsstatus.SDT is also set to 0.

## 3.1.6.3. Base ISA Control in mstatus Register

For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and Umode, respectively. The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa ,  shown in Table 11. The effective XLEN in S-mode and U-mode are termed SXLEN and UXLEN , respectively.

When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

When MXLEN=64, if S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero. Otherwise, it is a WARL field

that encodes the current value of SXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.

When MXLEN=64, if U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero. Otherwise, it is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN. In particular, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.

Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN, and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register. Similarly, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

We require that operations always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.

<image>

To reduce hardware complexity, the architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have  XLEN  settings  less  than  or  equal  to  the  next-higher  privilege  mode.  In  practice,  such settings would almost always be a software bug, but machine operation is well-defined even in this case.

Some  HINT  instructions  are encoded  as integer computational  instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value, e.g., c.addi x8, 0 . When such a HINT is executed with XLEN < MXLEN  and  bits  MXLEN..XLEN  of  the  destination register not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined  whether  bits  MXLEN..XLEN  of  the  destination  register  are  unchanged  or  are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.

<image>

This  definition  allows  implementations  to  elide  register  write-back  for  some  HINTs,  while allowing  them  to  execute  other  HINTs  in  the  same  manner  as  other  integer  computational instructions. The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 2: In the mstatus register of an RV64 hart, the UXL field controls the effective XLEN for U-mode, called UXLEN.

RULE 3: The SXL and UXL fields of mstatus are WARL (Write Any, Read Legal) fields when their corresponding privilege modes are supported, subject to implementation-defined legal values.

RULE 4: The encodings of the mstatus SXL and UXL fields are the same as the encoding of the MXL field in the misa register.

RULE 5: When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist in mstatus.

RULE 6: When MXLEN=32, the absence of the mstatus SXL and UXL fields implies SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.

RULE 7: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, the mstatus SXL field is read-only zero.

RULE 8: When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is supported, the mstatus SXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current SXLEN.

RULE 9: An implementation may make the mstatus SXL field read-only, provided its value always causes SXLEN=MXLEN.

RULE 10: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is not supported, the mstatus UXL field is read-only zero.

RULE 11: When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is supported, the mstatus UXL field is a WARL field that encodes the current UXLEN.

RULE 12: An implementation may make the mstatus UXL field read-only, provided its value always causes either UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.

RULE 13: If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal mstatus UXL values must exclude every value that would make UXLEN greater than SXLEN.

RULE 14: If an mstatus XLEN-control field configures a mode's XLEN to less than the widest supported XLEN, operations in that mode must ignore source-register bits above the configured XLEN.

RULE 15: If an mstatus XLEN-control field configures a mode's XLEN to less than the widest supported XLEN, operation results must be sign-extended to fill the entire widest supported destination register.

RULE 16: If an mstatus XLEN-control field configures a mode's XLEN to less than the widest supported XLEN, program-counter bits above that XLEN must be ignored.

RULE 17: When the program counter is written under an XLEN selected through mstatus, the written value must be sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.

RULE 18: Operations affected by the mstatus XLEN configuration must fill the entire underlying hardware register with defined values, avoiding implementation-defined values in otherwise unused upper bits.

RULE 19: Apart from constraints on legal WARL values such as the mstatus UXL restriction when S-mode is implemented, the architecture does not require hardware checks that a lower-privilege mode's XLEN is less than or equal to the XLEN of the next-higher privilege mode.

RULE 20: An unusual privilege-mode XLEN combination selected through mstatus, such as a lower-privilege mode having a larger XLEN than a higher-privilege mode, is generally likely to be a software bug, but machine operation remains well-defined.

RULE 21: Some HINT instructions are encoded as integer computational instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value, and their upper-bit behavior can depend on the current XLEN configured through mstatus.

RULE 22: An example of a HINT affected by an mstatus-selected XLEN is `c.addi x8, 0`, which nominally writes the current value of its destination register back to that register.

RULE 23: When a HINT executes with an mstatus-selected current XLEN less than MXLEN, and the destination-register bits above XLEN are not all equal to bit XLEN−1, the treatment of those upper bits is implementation-defined.

RULE 24: In the HINT case governed by an mstatus-selected XLEN less than MXLEN, an implementation may leave destination-register bits above XLEN unchanged.

RULE 25: In the HINT case governed by an mstatus-selected XLEN less than MXLEN, an implementation may instead overwrite destination-register bits above XLEN with copies of bit XLEN−1.

RULE 26: The implementation-defined HINT behavior associated with an mstatus-selected XLEN allows implementations to omit register write-back for some HINT instructions.

RULE 27: The implementation-defined HINT behavior associated with an mstatus-selected XLEN also allows implementations to execute other HINT instructions in the same way as ordinary integer computational instructions.

RULE 28: The implementation choice for upper destination-register bits after such a HINT is observable only by a privilege mode whose XLEN setting in mstatus is greater than the XLEN of the mode that executed the HINT.

RULE 29: The implementation choice for upper destination-register bits after such a HINT is invisible to the current privilege mode configured by mstatus.
